  ```sql
  -- The interval comparison is what makes this idempotent: a row already at
  -- 60 days does not match.
  update guest_commitments
  set expires_at = created_at + interval '60 days'
  where type in ('comp', 'discount')
    and status = 'open'
    and expires_at = created_at + interval '2 years';
  ```
  **Check what this would expire before running it.** Shortening a horizon can make a row elapsed the moment the statement commits, and the next lifecycle tick will then escalate and expire it in the same pass. That is correct behaviour, but it should not be a surprise:
  ```sql
  select id, description, created_at, created_at + interval '60 days' as new_expiry,
         (created_at + interval '60 days') <= now() as would_expire_now
  from guest_commitments
  where type in ('comp', 'discount') and status = 'open';
  ```

  Verify the recommendation rows were untouched — this is the regression check for the scope cut, and it is cheap:
  ```sql
  select type, status, count(*), count(expires_at) as with_horizon
  from guest_commitments group by 1, 2 order by 1, 2;
  ```
  `recommendation` rows must show `with_horizon = 0`. Le Mil's two open recommendations (guests ending 0853 and 7034) are the specific rows to eyeball.

- **Setting a venue's capability (`venue_info.services`, TAC-301 part 2).** There is no write path in the product yet — not the venue config page, not the seed flow, not extraction. It is hand-edited in Studio, and the field is what the now-conditional `hold` commitment type resolves against, so a venue with no `services` key offers no holds and states nothing either way about the rest. **Absent is not the same as `false`**: absent renders nothing, `false` renders an explicit "NOT available" line. Only write `false` for something the venue genuinely does not do.
  ```sql
  -- Le Mil's: counter-only, walk-in, no ahead-ordering, no holds.
  -- Type the slug manually. Merges into the existing venue_info rather than
  -- replacing it — venue_info has sibling keys a whole-object write would drop.
  update venue_configs
  set venue_info = venue_info || jsonb_build_object(
    'services', jsonb_build_object(
      'aheadOrdering', false,
      'holds', false,
      'reservations', false,
      'delivery', false,
      'alsoOffers', '[]'::jsonb,
      'alsoDoesNotOffer', '[]'::jsonb
    )
  )
  where venue_id = (select id from venues where slug = '<slug>');
  ```
  A malformed value degrades to absent rather than breaking the venue (`.catch(undefined)` on the field), so a typo costs the block silently rather than crashing every agent run — which also means **a typo is invisible**. Read the rendered block back on the venue's next reply, or re-select the column, rather than assuming the write took.
- **Holding every outbound for a venue (migration 031).** `venues.hold_all_outbound` defaults `false`. Set it to queue EVERY guest-facing content message from that venue for operator review — the `hold_all_outbound` trigger (#6 in "Approval policy gates"). `opt_out` confirmations still auto-send (TCPA/carrier compliance). **There is no toggle for this anywhere in the product** — not in the Command Center, not in the operator app, not in the seed flow. Every `from('venues')` call in the repo is a `.select()`; this column has no write path in code. Flip it by hand:
  ```sql
  -- Hold all outbound for one venue. Type the slug manually — do not
  -- interpolate — so this template can't be copy-pasted against the wrong row.
  update venues
  set hold_all_outbound = true
  where slug = '<slug>';
  ```
  Un-setting is the same statement with `false`. Read fresh every agent run, so it takes effect on the next inbound. **Do not confuse with `venue_configs.approval_policy`** — see the next gotcha.
- **`venue_configs.approval_policy` is READ and, since TAC-307, EDITABLE from Command Center.** The paragraph below records the TAC-343 decision to leave it uneditable; that call was reversed by TAC-307 when the Le Mil's pilot needed per-venue policy set without hand-written SQL. The surface is the "Approval policy" section on `/admin/venues/[slug]`, writing via `PATCH /admin/venues/api/venues/[venueId]/approval-policy`. Kept below because the *reasoning* still explains why it stayed unbuilt for so long. Historical: **it intentionally had no editing surface anywhere (TAC-343).** This entry previously said the column was "DEAD CONFIG — nothing reads it"; that was overtaken by the v1.24.0 change documented under "Warm-but-gated complaint handling" above — `parseApprovalPolicy` has been the first and only reader since, routing `comp_complaint` to `operator_approval`, fail-OPEN to `APPROVAL_POLICY_DEFAULT` on null/malformed. `scripts/onboarding/seed-supabase.ts` still writes the same seed-time literal (`{ default: 'auto_send', perCategory: {} }`) into every venue. **This is a deliberate product decision, not a gap**: TAC-343's investigation flagged that the column has no editing surface anywhere (not Voices, not the venue config page), and the call was to leave it that way — approval behavior is standard across venues, a property of how approvals were built rather than something an individual café tunes, so it doesn't need a per-venue editor and doesn't get its own section on the venue config page. It does get read-only visibility in that page's Readiness panel (a plain statement of the venue's current policy), so nothing the agent reads stays invisible. If a venue ever genuinely needs to differ, that's a new ticket, decided when it's actually hit — don't build speculative editing for it now. Unrelated but still true: `venues.hold_all_outbound` (migration 031) is a separate, Studio-only blanket-hold switch — don't conflate the two.
- **`MessageCategory` is DERIVED from `MESSAGE_CATEGORIES`, not hand-written (TAC-307).** `lib/ai/types.ts` exports `MESSAGE_CATEGORIES` as an `as const` array and defines `export type MessageCategory = (typeof MESSAGE_CATEGORIES)[number]`. It used to be a hand-written union with no companion array, which meant the category set could be checked at compile time but **not enumerated at runtime** — so any UI that needed one control per category had to carry its own copy of the list and drift from it silently. Add a category to the array and it appears in the Command Center approval-policy surface with no change to that surface. **Order in the array is meaningful**: it drives render order there, inbound-classifiable categories first, then outbound-only. Two consequences worth knowing: a record keyed on `z.enum(MESSAGE_CATEGORIES)` is EXHAUSTIVE in Zod and will reject any object that omits a category (the approval-policy write route hit this and validates keys in a separate pass instead), and `lib/schemas/approval-policy.ts`'s `PerCategorySchema` still deliberately keys on a loose `z.string()` at the LIVE boundary — see its own note for why.
- **Sets keyed on approval triggers must be TOTAL maps, not re-listed literals.** Any construct keyed on `APPROVAL_TRIGGERS` — push fire/skip decisions, operator-facing labels, analytics buckets — must be typed `satisfies Record<ApprovalTrigger, …>` so `tsc` refuses to compile until a newly added trigger gets an explicit decision. `ApprovalTrigger` is DERIVED (`(typeof APPROVAL_TRIGGERS)[keyof typeof APPROVAL_TRIGGERS]`), so adding a member to the const object widens the type automatically and breaks every total map — that is the mechanism doing the work. Where a total map genuinely doesn't fit (an optional display label), use `satisfies Partial<Record<ApprovalTrigger, …>>` so key typos still fail, and make sure the unmapped path degrades visibly rather than silently.
  **Both halves of this rule are written from failures.** `lib/operator/queue.ts` types its label map `Record<ApprovalTrigger | ExtraReviewReason, string>` and has NEVER drifted — TAC-297 (`ff653be`) and `0c1515c` both added their labels because the compiler refused otherwise. `lib/notifications/send.ts` derived its push allow-list from `Object.keys()` of an untyped `Record<string, string>` label map, so the same two commits added triggers that the push layer silently dropped: **no APNs request was attempted between 2026-06-01 and the fix**, while 121 unit tests stayed green — one of which actively asserted the broken behavior (`returns false for unknown triggers`). Same repo, same hazard, one typed and one not.
  Corollary: when a lookup drives a *decision*, default to the safe-loud side. `shouldSendDraftFlaggedPush` now fails OPEN — an unrecognized trigger pushes rather than being dropped, because a dismissible push beats a queued draft nobody was told about.
- **The split delimiter dual-source-of-truth pair is DISSOLVED (TAC-319), and the absence is now the asserted state.** TAC-313 paired `SYSTEM_TEMPLATE`'s R12 (emit `[[BREAK]]`) with `BUBBLE_DELIMITER` + `DELIMITER_PATTERN` in `lib/agent/split-message.ts`; TAC-319 deleted R12, so the prompt must carry NO trace of the token — `system-template.test.ts` now asserts its ABSENCE, while `split-message.test.ts` still owns the matcher, which survives purely as a defensive stripper (legacy-era traces and stray model output). Do not reintroduce the token into any prompt layer without reopening the whole TAC-319 decision; splitting is decided in `lib/agent/sentence-split.ts` at dispatch.
  The matcher is **deliberately over-tolerant, and the direction is the point**: it accepts `[BREAK]`, `[[break]]`, `[[ BREAK ]]`, `[[BREAK]`, `[[BREAK` fused to the next word. Post-TAC-319 a false positive merely strips a token that shouldn't be there anyway (no wrong text shown); a false negative ships `[[BREAK]` to a guest. Two shapes are deliberately NOT matched: a bare unbracketed `BREAK` (appears in ordinary prose — "we take a break at three"), and a SINGLE-bracket token fused to the next word, because matching that would mean giving up the `[BREAKFAST]` protection. Adding tolerance without re-checking `[BREAKFAST]` would start corrupting real menu text.
- **`RecentMessage.delivery` is required (TAC-394).** Every `RecentMessage` fixture needs `delivery: 'delivered'` (or the unsent value under test). Deliberate: a delivery field that defaults to delivered when omitted reproduces the defect. The history query's two delivery columns (`status`, `review_state`) are pinned by a SOURCE-level test in `build-runtime-context.test.ts`, because nothing runs `buildRuntimeContext` for real under test and a Supabase mock ignores its `select()` argument. Drop a column and every row reads its default with no other test failing.
- **The obligation-slot type list lives in SQL AND TypeScript, and the two move together (TAC-394).** Migration 041's index predicates hard-code `('comp', 'hold', 'discount')`; `pendingSlotOf` (`lib/agent/pending-slots.ts`) derives the same set from `OBLIGATION_TYPES`. Changing `OBLIGATION_TYPES` without a migration recreating both indexes makes the app and the index disagree about which slot a row is in: the app regenerates into one slot while the index enforces the other, and the symptom is a unique-violation red alert on a turn that should have queued. `pending-slots.test.ts` reads the migration file and fails if the lists differ. `lib/agent/testing/pending-rows-fake.ts` deliberately writes the list out from the migration rather than importing it, because a fake that reused the code under test would agree with it by construction.
- **Per-guest pending-draft reads go through `loadPendingRowsBySlot`, and a source-level guard enforces it (TAC-394).** A guest can hold two pending cards, so a single-row `review_state='pending'` read (`.limit(1)` or `.maybeSingle()`) returns an arbitrary one of them, and a read that picks the wrong slot regenerates the wrong card. `pending-slots.test.ts` scans `lib/`, `app/` and `scripts/` for such reads and allows exactly one: `findPendingQuestion`, which must order by `created_at` ascending. The guard's self-tests include equivalent spellings (both quote styles, a multi-line chain, `.match()`, `.filter()`, an `.or()` leg), per the source-level guard gotcha above. `loadPendingRowsBySlot` fails OPEN (null), and a third pending row is reported as `pending_slot_invariant_broken` rather than silently picked over. **Known limit:** `findPendingQuestion` renders only the OLDEST knowledge-gap card when each slot holds one; rendering both is a prompt change with its own measurement run. **The guard walks the filesystem, not git**, so a gitignored script with such a read fails it locally while CI stays green; on 2026-09-15 that was `scripts/sandbox/uat-tac-258-d8.ts` (TAC-395 finding).
- **The new pending-slot code must never run against migration 020 (TAC-394).** It cannot overwrite a card there, but every second card fails: the INSERT hits 020's index, recovery finds the target slot empty and retries, and after `RACE_RECOVERY_MAX_ATTEMPTS` the turn ends in a red alert with no reply to the guest. `two-pending-slots.test.ts` pins exactly that against an in-memory table enforcing 020. That is why 041 applies before merge, and why a reverted deploy (old code against 041) counts as the deploy window again: old code's unordered `.limit(1)` read can pick the OTHER slot's card and move it into an occupied slot, which is a unique violation and a failed turn.
- **The reached-guest condition is written in three places, and a test binds them (TAC-395).** Which messages reached the guest (inbound, or outbound whose `review_state` is not pending and whose `status` is `sending`, `sent` or `delivered`) is enforced in `lib/operator/thread.ts`, as a PostgREST `.or()` built from `DELIVERED_OUTBOUND_STATUSES`, and in migrations 043 (conversations list) and 044 (queue `recent_context`). It has to run in SQL, before the 200-row cap and the 3-response limit, so it can't be one TypeScript function. `lib/operator/reached-guest-condition.test.ts` fails if either migration's status list drifts from the constant, if the constant disagrees with `deriveDelivery` on any direction, status and review_state, or if `thread.ts` hardcodes the list. **So changing `DELIVERED_OUTBOUND_STATUSES` needs a migration recreating both functions, and repointing that test at the new migration, since it reads 043 and 044 by name.** Two traps: the PostgREST form of `review_state IS DISTINCT FROM 'pending'` is `or(review_state.is.null,review_state.neq.pending)`, because a bare `neq` drops rows whose `review_state` is NULL; and `recent_context` deliberately has no `body <> ''` (22:08 ruling: a photo-only text is context the operator needs), so don't align it with the thread endpoints. The migration checks pin each clause's exact statement fragment inside its CTE or lateral, so a clause that is dropped, moved or re-parenthesised fails. A first version checked loose substrings, and code review found five wrong migrations that passed it, including a 044 with its parentheses dropped that would have filled every card's context with other guests' messages.
- **A local `npx vitest run` and `npm run lint` also collect `.claude/worktrees/**` (TAC-395).** Neither `vitest.config.ts` nor the ESLint config excludes it, so every leftover git worktree adds a full copy of the repo. On 2026-09-15 three worktrees took the local test run from 196 files to 778, with five extra failing files from stale branches, and produced 89,728 lint problems, every one inside the worktrees. CI checks out clean and never sees them. Measure tests with `npx vitest run --exclude '.claude/**'` and lint changed files directly (`npx eslint <files>`); don't read an unexcluded local run as this repo's result.
- **`groupIntoResponses` requires `created_at DESC` input and fails SILENTLY if given anything else (TAC-313).** It selects the first N groups it encounters, so oldest-first input keeps the OLDEST responses and drops the newest — no error, no type change, and every contiguous fixture still looks right. The live caller orders DESC in the query; `group-responses.test.ts` pins the precondition with an explicit reversed-input assertion. This bit during development, which is why the test exists. Related: grouping is **keyed, not adjacent** — a guest can text inside the inter-bubble gap, putting an inbound row between two bubbles of one response, so an adjacency-based merge would pass every fixture and mis-render in production.
- **There is NO pre-send pause, and `lib/agent/timing.ts` no longer exists (TAC-421).** `scheduleAndSend` used to call `sampleTiming()` and sleep before marking as read. TAC-420 measured what that cost: the sleep ran AFTER generation, the backstops and the approval gate, so it added a mean **6497ms** (re-measured at n=50,000; TAC-420 reported 6506 at n=20,000) to a pipeline already taking 14–17s — **27% of the 24.4s post-backstop first-bubble p50**. It fired on exactly two paths, non-demo inbound auto-send and non-demo engine followups, and on the followup path `ctx.currentMessage` is null so the wait was not even followed by a read receipt. `markAsRead` → typing indicator → send still run in that order, with nothing between them. The module, `TimingPlan`, its four tunables and `timing.test.ts` are all gone; `INTER_BUBBLE_GAP_MS` (`split-message.ts`) is untouched and is the only sleep left in the function, which is exactly what TAC-313 §6's refusal to derive it from the opening delay bought. **The lesson from the deleted code is worth keeping even though the specimen is gone:** `TOTAL_DELAY_*` (500–1000ms) was smaller than `MARK_AS_READ_GAP_*` (5000–8000ms), so `remainingAfterRead` was always negative, both derived pauses clamped to 0 at `setTimeout`, and the docstring described a 30–60s window that never existed. `timing.test.ts` certified it, and the mechanism is worth stating precisely because a first version of this entry got it wrong: the invariant test did not pass *because* the negatives cancelled, it was a **tautology** — `typingDurationMs` was DEFINED as `totalDelayMs - markAsReadGapMs - preTypingPauseMs`, so re-adding the three terms returned `totalDelayMs` for any values of any sign, and the assertion could not fail for any implementation. Its sibling named `'totalDelayMs always falls in [30s, 60s]'` asserted `>=500 && <=1000` — one of this file's canonical specimens of a test name that is not evidence of what the test checks (the others, both still live, are the `future-add safety` push test and `comp_regex_backstop`). A third, never-logged instance in the same file: `'markAsReadGapMs always falls in [5s, 15s]'`, whose **upper bound** asserted `<= 15_000` against a value that could not exceed 8000 and so could not fail; its `>= 5_000` half was live against `MARK_AS_READ_GAP_MIN_MS`, so the test as a whole could fail even though half of it was decorative.
- **The grounding verifier's source material must track the GENERATOR's prompt, or correct replies get suppressed (TAC-301 part 1.5).** `lib/ai/verify-grounding.ts` checks a drafted reply against what the assistant knew. If a fact-bearing block reaches the generator and not the verifier, a CORRECT reply reads as an unsupported claim and the backstop queues it — the failure is invisible in tests because both halves work in isolation. Six such divergences shipped before anyone noticed, and only one ever fired in production. The seam is now `runtimeContext: generation.userPrompt`, passed **verbatim**. Two rules follow. **Never rebuild that string from `ctx`, and never pass a curated subset** — the identity is the whole guarantee, and a maintained list is what fell behind four times. **A new fact-bearing runtime block needs no verifier change at all** (it rides the prompt automatically), but `verify-grounding.test.ts`'s block-coverage test exists to catch the case where someone reintroduces curation; if you find yourself editing that test to REMOVE a header, stop and check you haven't just recreated the bug. Related and non-obvious: that same test file pins the output schema's FIELD ORDER, because `reasoning` must generate before `hasUngroundedClaim` — see the runtime-contract entry for the reasoning-reverses-itself case.
- **`guests.is_test_synthetic` is a live flag that changes NOTHING at runtime.** It is `true` on the primary Mock Sextant test record (`+17869530853`), reads like it should alter agent behavior, and does not: its only consumers are `scripts/onboarding/run-test-scenarios.ts` (seeding + re-seed guard) and the generated `db/types.ts`. Nothing in `lib/agent`, the webhook path, or the approval gate reads it. Its documented purpose is analytics filtering. **Do not reason about send/queue behavior from it, and do not add behavior to it without also removing the ambiguity.**
  This is the third instance this session of a name promising behavior it does not have, and the pattern is worth naming: `venue_configs.approval_policy` sat seeded fleet-wide with zero readers for 102 days while looking exactly like where per-category approval routing lived (it now is, as of v1.24.0); the `future-add safety` test asserted the opposite of its name for two months; and this flag reads as a runtime switch while being inert. **When you find a well-named thing, confirm it has a reader before you reason from it** — `grep -rn '<name>' --include='*.ts' . | grep -v test` costs seconds and each of these three cost hours.
- **A safety gate's green signal is only evidence if you have confirmed what it fires on.** A gate that has never fired, or has fired only on inputs you never inspected, is UNPROVEN — not working. Before trusting one, query its firings and read them.
  Two instances, both of which generated confidence for months:
  - `comp_regex_backstop` (TAC-212) fired **exactly once** in its lifetime, and that hit matched the model **refusing** a refund: *"A **refund** isn't something I can do over text."* The regex caught the word `refund` inside a denial. A 100% false-positive rate on its only data point, read for two months as a working comp backstop, while every actual in-kind comp sailed past it. The 2026-08-07 incident is what that illusion cost.
  - The `returns false for unknown triggers (future-add safety)` test (fixed in `f7cdb22`) asserted the exact opposite of its name and passed on every CI run for two months while the push fire-set silently dropped two new triggers.
  Same failure class: a signal that *looks* like coverage. The generalization — **distrust any gate, test, or alarm whose true-positive history you cannot produce on demand.** Corollary for reviewers: "the tests pass" and "the backstop is in place" are claims about mechanism, not about outcomes; ask what it caught, and when.
  **And the part worth internalizing more than the fix: a test asserting a defensive default is only as good as the DIRECTION of that default, and a test name is not evidence of what the test checks.** The suite did not merely fail to catch this regression — it asserted it, for two months, in green:
  ```ts
  it('returns false for unknown triggers (future-add safety)', () => {
    expect(shouldSendDraftFlaggedPush('something_new')).toBe(false)
  })
  ```
  The name promises future-add safety. The assertion encodes the exact opposite — any trigger the map doesn't recognize is silently discarded. When `ff653be` and `0c1515c` each added a trigger, both were "unknown triggers," both were dropped, and this test passed on every CI run *confirming that as intended behavior*. Scanning the name would have reassured a reviewer; only reading the assertion against the failure actually feared ("what happens the next time someone adds a trigger?") would have caught it. So: when writing a test for the unknown/default path, name the direction, not the intent — and say in the test body which direction is safe and why. If the safe direction is "do the thing, loudly," the assertion is `true`. A test that locks in silence is worse than no test, because it converts an omission into a documented decision.
- **A verifier verdict on ONE draft is a sample, and a sample of one supports no claim about the check (TAC-409).** `verifyGrounding` runs at temperature 0.2, not 0, so its verdict on a given body is a draw from a distribution, not a property of the body. Production records exactly one draw per draft, in `messages.ungrounded_claims`.
  **Measured, on the eight drafts this project spent a day reasoning from.** Replaying each body against its own frozen source material, the OLD prompt's hold rate was `a1345d56` **2 of 10**, `c7a01385` **5 of 10**, and the croissants negation `18e91c8a` **0 of 5** — that last one *never reproduced the flag production had recorded*, and it was not context drift (`venue_configs.updated_at` predates every draft, and the live staff roster matched each generator's own system prompt). Three of the eight had no stable OLD behaviour at all.
  **So "eight flagged drafts" described eight coin flips, not eight behaviours**, and every conclusion drawn from them — including the "one genuine catch in eight" framing that reframed the whole ticket — treated a single draw as a measurement. The defect was real; its rate was not what the flags implied.
  Three rules follow. **Never characterise the check from one verdict** — not from a queue card, not from a `ungrounded_claims` row, not from one replay. **Run a control arm**: a body that stops being flagged after a prompt change proves nothing unless the unchanged prompt flagged it consistently first, which is why any body the control holds inconsistently must be excluded from a before/after claim rather than counted either way. **And read the claim TEXT, not just the boolean** — on `c7a01385` the flag count barely moved (5/10 → 4/10) while the content changed completely, from omission complaints to a single genuine contradiction the omission noise had been masking. A count-only reading would have called that a failed fix.
  Generalizes past this verifier to every `generateObject` call whose output is treated as a finding: the classifier, `verify-mechanic-offer`, `extract-reported-order`. One output is an observation about one call.
- **TAC-308 adds a fourth required-with-optional-inner emission field: `knowledgeGap`.** Unlike `contextUpdate` / `commitment` / `arrivalCapture` it's a bare required boolean, so it costs ZERO against the TAC-300 optional-field budget (the counter counts optionals only; the schema stays at 20). **Implication for new fixtures / mocks:** every fresh `GenerateMessageResult` literal must now carry `knowledgeGap: false` alongside `contextUpdate: {}, commitment: {}, arrivalCapture: {}` or tsc fails. Every `RuntimeContext` fixture must carry `pendingQuestion: null`.
- **`messages.pending_until` is three-state at the persist layer, and the omission is load-bearing (TAC-308).** `persistOrRegenQueuedDraft`'s `pendingUntil` option: a `Date` stamps it, `undefined` means *leave the column alone* — omitted on INSERT (so it lands null), and absent from the UPDATE payload (so an existing clock SURVIVES). There is deliberately no "clear it" value: the ONLY thing that clears `pending_until` is the timer's CAS claim, which is what makes that claim the single writer of the fired/not-fired state. Two behaviors depend on the omission — a guest asking a second unanswerable question refreshes the card body but can't push its deadline out, and the post-holding card regen can't re-arm the clock it just fired (which would send a second holding message five minutes later). Contrast `pending_commitment` in the same UPDATE payload, which is overwritten wholesale by design (TAC-297 call #1). Same statement, opposite policies, deliberately.
- **`persistOrRegenQueuedDraft`'s INSERT-on-vanished-row recovery is WRONG for update-only callers — pass `updateOnly: true` (TAC-308).** The default race recovery (UPDATE misses → INSERT a fresh pending row) is right for the orchestrators, where the draft still needs somewhere to live. It is actively harmful for the timeout regen: if an operator approves the card during the seconds spent generating, an INSERT creates a phantom pending row answering a question the guest was already answered, carrying `review_reason='knowledge_gap'` (so `isKnowledgeGapCard` protects it forever, silently dropping later drafts), with `pending_until` null (so the timer never touches it) and no push (the orchestrators fire those, not the persist helper). Nothing surfaces it until the operator next opens the queue. Overloaded signature: only an `updateOnly` caller can be returned `action: 'skipped'`, so the other three call sites need no narrowing.
- **`isKnowledgeGapCard` takes BOTH `pending_until` and `review_reason`, and unknown means not-a-card (TAC-308).** The OR is load-bearing in both directions. `pending_until IS NOT NULL` catches a card whose label was won by a co-firing trigger; `review_reason='knowledge_gap'` catches a card whose clock has already fired, because the timer CLEARS `pending_until` as its claim — without that second condition the card would silently lose its eviction protection five minutes after being created, which is the original data-loss bug on a delay. The missing-field default is `typeof pending_until === 'string'`, NOT `!== null`, because an absent field is `undefined` and `undefined !== null` is true — which would classify every ordinary pending draft as a protected card and start silently dropping replies that used to send. Accepted residual: a draft that both gapped and committed a comp carries `review_reason='commitment_type_gated'`, so once its clock fires it is no longer recognized. Rare, and it degrades to pre-TAC-308 behavior. Closing it needs a column.
- **A knowledge-gap card is protected indefinitely, and the interaction with complaint routing is sharp (TAC-308).** There is no time bound on the protection and no escalation beyond the initial push. The case worth knowing: a guest with an outstanding card who then complains gets their clarifying turn auto-sent (the carve-out), but the warm apologetic *resolving* draft trips `category_requires_approval`. **Since TAC-394 it is dropped only when it lands in the slot the gap card holds.** A self-reported gap card is blank and sits in the conversation slot, so a resolving draft carrying a structured comp, hold or discount takes the obligation slot and queues beside it; a resolving draft with no carrier (an apology, or a comp promised only in prose) takes the gap card's slot and is DROPPED (`knowledge_gap_card_protected`). The operator's card then still shows the answer to the original question, and the apology draft they were supposed to authorize is gone. `captureDraftDropped` Slack-relays so this is visible rather than silent, but the underlying policy question — should the protection expire, or should complaint turns be exempt from the drop — is deliberately left open rather than decided unilaterally. Revisit if pilot surfaces it.
- **`MAX_OUTPUT_TOKENS` has now bitten twice, and the second time was invisible for weeks (TAC-309, TAC-367).** TAC-309 raised `generate-message`'s cap 500 → 1500 after a truncation took production generation down; TAC-367 found the identical shape in `verify-grounding` — successful calls emitting 373–496 tokens against a 500 cap, the best clearing it by four. **The pattern to watch for: an unbounded field (`reasoning`) declared EARLY in a structured-output schema, against a cap nobody revisits.** Declaration order is load-bearing for correctness in both cases (TAC-301 part 1.5 moved `reasoning` first so the verdict follows the analysis), which is exactly why the ordering change and the cap are easy to decouple — the ordering PR is about correctness and the cap looks unrelated. Two rules follow. **When you move an unbounded field earlier in an LLM output schema, re-measure the output-token distribution in the same PR.** And **when a call site swallows its own failures, a truncation there is invisible by construction** — `verifyGroundingStage` logged a `console.warn` and returned null, so nothing in PostHog, Slack, or Langfuse ever recorded that the check hadn't run. `captureGenerationTruncated` exists for generation precisely because of TAC-309; `captureGroundingVerifierUnavailable` is its TAC-367 sibling. Any new `generateObject` call site that degrades silently needs one of these before it ships, not after.
- **Reconstructing a past agent turn re-dates it: `historyEndIso` pins message history, NOT the clock (TAC-367).** `buildRuntimeContext` stamps a fresh `now` on every call, so replaying an old turn renders `## Right now` against TODAY's time and open/closed state. A reply that correctly said "we open at 7 tomorrow" when it was sent reads as a factual error when re-verified hours later, and the grounding verifier — which is explicitly told the status line is the authority on what time it is — flags it. Measured: replaying 18 real Le Mil's replies produced flagged claims on two that had shipped `auto_sent` in production, entirely from this. **Do not read a flag from a reconstructed turn as a production finding**, and if you need a faithful replay, note that nothing currently lets you inject the clock. This is the same class as the `run-test-scenarios` open/closed caveat (TAC-301) — a run's results aren't comparable across a change in venue open state. **Delivery markers re-date the same way (TAC-394):** `historyEndIso` pins which rows load, but each row's `status` and `review_state` are read as they are now, so a draft that was pending at the original turn and approved later renders as delivered in the Voices playground.
- **`guest_intention_prompts` holds STATE rows since migration 040, and five traps follow (TAC-380).** Each has a test written before the migration file. (1) **A reader keyed on row EXISTENCE reads every eligible intention as already asked** — renders nothing, records nothing, green suite. Readers filter `prompted_at IS NOT NULL` in SQL (`lib/agent/intentions/load.ts`), so a null result still means only "a read failed". (2) **An `ignoreDuplicates` upsert (ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING) cannot stamp an existing eligibility row**, so the stamp is an UPDATE guarded `.is('prompted_at', null)`. `ignoreDuplicates` is still right for the eligibility INSERT: it keeps the earliest `eligible_at`, so a chatty guest can't slide a window later. (3) **`prompted_at` keeps its `DEFAULT now()` through the deploy window** (TAC-392 drops it afterwards), so an insert that merely omits the column is born prompted; `buildEligibilityRow` writes explicit nulls, pinned with `toStrictEqual` because a missing key and a null differ. (4) **Pessimistic closure closes whatever recording is handed**, so it must be handed `renderableIntentions(...)`, never `ctx.openIntentions`. (5) **The Command Center reads the same table**: `loadIntentionPrompts` filters `prompted_at IS NOT NULL`, `loadVenueOpenIntentions` filters `IS NULL`.
- **Re-run migration 040's backfill block after deploy (TAC-380).** Apply 040 before merge, then re-run the block between its markers once the deploy is live. Rows the OLD code writes in between carry a `learn_first_order` key, no `eligible_at` and no `prompt_source`. Every statement is idempotent. Until the re-run, the derivation reads a legacy `learn_first_order` row as `understand_order` (`resolveIntentionKey`, `definitions.ts`), so a guest asked in that window is not asked again — but the re-run is still required, because the Command Center and the backfill's own bookkeeping read the raw key. When re-running the block on its own, wrap it in `begin`/`commit`: an eligibility insert landing between its DELETE and its RENAME can hit the unique constraint, which is harmless but fails that statement.
- **The intention brake can only judge prompts whose answer it can see (TAC-380).** `inboundTimes` comes from `recentMessages` — `MAX_HISTORY_DAYS`, capped at `MAX_HISTORY_MESSAGES` responses — plus the current inbound, and `build-runtime-context.ts` passes where that history starts as `inboundHistoryFrom`. A prompt older than it is LEFT OUT of the streak. Counting it as unanswered would brake a guest who answered everything, and the brake cannot lift on its own: nothing renders, so no newer prompt is ever recorded. Found in self-review before merge; the first version failed toward braking, against ruling 1's under-braking direction. The horizon is computed by the pure `resolveInboundHistoryFrom` (`lib/agent/intentions/derive.ts`), tested directly, and `lib/agent/build-runtime-context.test.ts` pins the call site at the source level, because nothing runs `buildRuntimeContext` for real under test. **Three accepted limits, recorded rather than fixed:** (1) once engaged, the brake lifts only by AGEING OUT — nothing renders, so no newer answered prompt is recorded, and the unanswered pair must fall outside the loaded history (up to 14 days) first, while first-contact windows keep running; (2) a photo-only reply is not an answer, because the history query excludes empty bodies and a media-only inbound is stored with `body = ''`; (3) the Voices regen playground can engage the brake where production did not, because regen pins history to `historyEndIso` but loads today's prompt rows.
- **A re-arm and the prompt stamp are ordered by guards, not by timing, and a prompted event-armed row can be OPEN (TAC-380).** Both writes are fire-and-forget on the same turn. The re-arm is an UPDATE of `eligible_at` alone, guarded `eligible_at < new anchor`; it never touches the prompt. The stamp for the two event-armed intentions also sets `eligible_at` to the anchor that rendered, one row at a time, guarded `eligible_at <= anchor AND (prompted_at IS NULL OR prompted_at < anchor)`. Whichever lands first, the re-arm never reopens the arming that was just asked about, a stamp from a superseded arming never closes the newer one, and a second stamp on the same arming keeps the first prompt's `message_id`; `record.test.ts` runs those orderings against an in-memory row rather than only pinning filter shapes. First-contact keys keep the plain `prompted_at IS NULL` batch stamp and never reopen. **Readers must not treat `prompted_at IS NOT NULL` as closed for the two event-armed keys**, which is trap 1 turned around: `deriveIntentionState` reopens a row whose prompt predates its anchor, and `loadVenueOpenIntentions` runs a second read for those keys because PostgREST can't compare two columns. Accepted limit: one row per intention holds only its LATEST prompt, so a later prompt overwrites an earlier one the brake could have counted. That can over-brake a guest whose earlier answer was overwritten, and it lifts as prompts age out of the visible history. And the mid-conversation hold reads the NEWEST event, so a guest who orders more often than once per conversation window (every other day at the 48-hour default) never has a newest order old enough to arm `did_they_like_it`, and an already-open row can run out while held. That fails toward not asking and follows ruling 2; the visit history's 20-row cap only matters inside that same case. The hold also reads recommendations' `updated_at`, because a repeated recommendation is deduped onto its old row (TAC-318) and bumps only that; any other write to the row bumps it too, which only holds more often.
- **`learn_name` closes on ANY string in `guests.first_name` (TAC-380 Probe 1).** `updateGuestContext` writes the field verbatim and THE-157 rules out length constraints on the LLM-facing schema, so an arbitrary string a guest offers as a name closes the intention permanently, as well as rendering into every later prompt. Documented and accepted, not fixed.
- **Test-harness runs are not comparable across TAC-380's deploy.** v1.49.0 changes what a scenario can raise underneath it: seven intentions, a different gate, R31's carve-out. Read a `run-test-scenarios` diff across the bump as a baseline reset, never as a regression. The harness builds context but never records eligibility, so synthetic guests re-derive every intention as newly eligible on every run.
- **Le Mil's `venue_configs.followup_rules` is NULL** and runs on `FOLLOWUP_RULES_DEFAULT`, so its intention brake window is the 48h code default. Retuning `recent_conversation_hours` for followups moves the brake too, by design (TAC-380 ruling 1).
- **`guests.context` writes are last-write-wins (TAC-296).** Two near-simultaneous inbounds from the same guest at the same venue both call `getGuestContext` against the same baseline, deep-merge their respective patches, and race on the final UPDATE. The second write clobbers the first; the losing patch's data is lost. **Why we accept this for v1:** pilot scale rarely produces concurrent inbounds from a single guest, and the lost write is recoverable (the agent re-captures on the next conversation). **When to revisit:** if pilot data shows the pattern, add optimistic locking via `guests.updated_at` precondition + retry-on-conflict, mirroring the TAC-264 partial-unique-index approach for `messages.review_state='pending'`. Don't ship that complexity until the failure mode is observed.
- **Reading `guests.context` directly via SQL or jsonb paths is brittle.** Always go through `GuestContextSchema.safeParse` (or `getGuestContext` from `lib/guests/context.ts`). The schema is permissive (unknown keys stripped, no `.strict()`), so future migrations can extend the shape without breaking older deploys — but raw `->` / `->>` paths in SQL or app code bypass `filterActiveLifeContext` (expired entries leak into the prompt) and the observations-truncation-to-10. If you need a SQL surface (e.g. a Command Center read-only viewer), call `getGuestContext` from the route handler and pass the parsed shape; don't render JSONB paths raw.
- **`contextUpdate` is a required schema field, inner shape is `.optional()`.** Per the TAC-212 precedent (Anthropic's structured-output validator is more reliable with explicit field presence), `GeneratedMessageSchema.contextUpdate` must be present on every emission — but both inner sub-fields (`structured`, `observation`) are `.optional()` so the no-op shape is `contextUpdate: {}`. The orchestrator's `isEmptyContextUpdate` check short-circuits before any DB hit. **Implication for tests / mocks:** any new fixture that constructs a `GenerateMessageResult` MUST include `contextUpdate: {}` or the typecheck fails. Existing fixtures (`stages.test.ts`, `schedule-and-send.test.ts`, etc.) were updated in the TAC-296 ship; new ones inherit the same requirement.
- **TAC-297 adds two more required-with-optional-inner schema fields: `commitment` and `arrivalCapture`.** Same Anthropic-structured-output reasoning as `contextUpdate` — both must be present on every emission, both have all-optional inner shapes (no-op shapes are `commitment: {}` and `arrivalCapture: {}`). Helpers `isEmptyCommitmentEmission` + `isEmptyArrivalCapture` short-circuit; `pendingFromEmission` returns null on no-op. **Implication for new fixtures / mocks:** every fresh `GenerateMessageResult` literal must carry `contextUpdate: {}, commitment: {}, arrivalCapture: {}` or tsc fails. Existing TAC-296 fixtures were extended in the TAC-297 ship.
- **`GeneratedMessageSchema` optional-field budget (TAC-300).** Anthropic's structured-output validator rejects tool schemas with more than 24 optional parameters across the entire nested tree ("Schemas contains too many optional parameters … limit: 24"). The CI guardrail in `lib/ai/schema-budget.test.ts` walks the schema's ZodOptional wrappers and fails CI if the count exceeds 22 (a 2-slot buffer below the cap so a single near-term field can land without a coordinated reshape). The TAC-296 → TAC-297 accumulation hit 31 and took production generation down for ~hours on 2026-05-30 — TAC-300 reshaped `GuestContextPatchSchema` (collapsed `home_base`/`workplace` to bare strings, dropped `pronouns`/`date_of_birth`/`observations` from the patch, dropped patch `captured_at`) to land at 20. **When adding a new agent-emission field:** a required boolean/string is free (Anthropic counts optionals only); a single optional leaf is 1 slot; an optional nested object is 1 slot for the object PLUS one for each optional leaf inside (`home_base: { neighborhood?, zip?, city?, address? }` was 5 slots, not 1). If the new field would push the count over 22, reshape first (collapse to a single optional string, fold into the `observation` free-text shortcut, or split into a separate tool call) BEFORE merging. The guardrail counter is OUR reconstruction of Anthropic's rule — it matched the validator's reported 31 on the pre-fix schema, but the real proof is the prod smoke test on every schema change.
- **`GuestContextPatchSchema` (LLM-facing) and `GuestContextSchema` (persisted read) intentionally diverge post-TAC-300.** PATCH side is slim: `guest_details` carries only `first_name?, last_name?, home_base?: string, workplace?: string`; no `pronouns`/`date_of_birth`; no `observations` array (the `observation` string shortcut is the only append path); life_context entries carry only `note` and `expires_at?`. PERSISTED side is widened with `z.union([z.string(), LegacyHomeBaseSchema])` so pre-TAC-300 nested-object rows still parse on read. `toParsedGuestContext` normalizes the legacy object shape into a bare string for the prompt block (joins the legacy fields with ", "). Lazy migration: the next agent emission that touches `home_base`/`workplace` shallow-merges the new string over the legacy object, in place — no data backfill required. **Implication for new code:** never read `guests.context.guest_details.home_base.neighborhood` directly via SQL or jsonb paths — always go through `getGuestContext` → `toParsedGuestContext` so the normalization fires. Pronouns / date_of_birth still surface on the persisted shape and the serializer (capturable-before-stays-capturable-after); new captures route through the `observation` shortcut.
- **`messages.pending_commitment` carrier writes are OVERWRITTEN on regen, not preserved (TAC-297 design call #1).** The TAC-264 `persistOrRegenQueuedDraft` UPDATE path adds `pending_commitment` to the UPDATED column set: regen wholesale-replaces the carrier so it always reflects the current draft's intent. If the new generation has no commitment, the carrier is nulled. Consequence: an operator who looked at the operator-card preview before regen might see a stale "comp committed" annotation that disappears on the next live refresh — that's correct behavior (the new draft no longer commits the comp). The TAC-298 Realtime subscription on `messages` is the live-refresh anchor.
- **Reading `messages.pending_commitment` must go through `PendingCommitmentSchema`.** Raw `->>` / `#>>` SQL paths into the jsonb risk producing nulls when the model returned an unexpected shape (the column accepts any jsonb at the storage layer; the schema enforcement happens at the read boundary in `lib/operator/dispatch-operator-outbound.ts`). On `PendingCommitmentSchema.safeParse` failure the dispatch helper LOGS + SKIPS the materialization — the message has been sent; the commitment row just doesn't get created. Reconciliation is a future ticket; for now: any new reader of this column must call `safeParse` before consuming the fields.
- **TAC-297 push surfaces are CAS-rowcount-gated, not status-rechecked.** `sendCommitmentArrivalPush` assumes the caller has already won the `transitionToPendingAck` CAS (rowcount=1). It does NOT re-check the row's status before firing — calling it from anywhere other than a CAS-win path would double-push. The two live callers (`handle-inbound.ts` for imminent, `commitments-due.ts` for scheduled) both gate on `transitioned=true` from the helper's return shape. Any future call site must do the same.
- **TAC-297 helper duplication in `lib/notifications/`.** `loadRecipients`, `nullOperatorToken`, and the per-operator badge count are duplicated between `send.ts` (draft-flagged) and `send-commitment-push.ts` (commitment-arrival). The badge function differs: `send-commitment-push.ts` sums pending drafts + pending_ack commitments (single source of truth across both surfaces); `send.ts` still only counts drafts. **If the badge predicate ever changes (e.g., new pending surface), update both files.** Extraction to a shared `lib/notifications/recipients.ts` module is a known follow-up.
- **TAC-299 persist-not-send is structurally enforced — DON'T add `scheduleAndSend` / `sendMessage` imports to `lib/agent/handle-operator-decline.ts`.** The operator-decline orchestrator's load-bearing invariant is that it persists a pending draft and NEVER dispatches to Sendblue. The structural test `handle-operator-decline.test.ts` parses the file's import set and fails if either `scheduleAndSend` or `sendMessage` (or `applyApprovalPolicyStage`) appears. A refactor that "consolidates" the orchestrator with `handle-followup.ts` by reusing its imports would silently regress this — the structural test is the canary. If you genuinely need to send from this surface (you probably don't — the operator does it via the edit screen + `editAndSend`), delete the structural assertions explicitly and document why in the same commit so a future reader can trace the decision.
- **TAC-299 `markCancelled` writes NO `cancelled_at` / `cancelled_by` columns by design.** The ticket was scoped to no-migration; the operator audit lives entirely on the `operator_draft_decline_initiated` PostHog event payload (`operatorId` + `commitmentId` + `messageId`). The CAS helper signature DOES accept `operatorId` for caller parity with `markAcknowledged` (so route handlers can pass the same opts shape), but it's intentionally unused inside the helper — see the comment on the field. If audit-column persistence becomes a requirement, add the columns via a migration **and** thread the value into the UPDATE; don't silently start writing what the schema can't accept.
- **TAC-123 `FollowupReason` widening requires THREE coordinated edits.** The enum is mirrored across (1) `lib/ai/types.ts` (`FollowupReason` union — the AI render-side enum), (2) `lib/schemas/followup-rules.ts` (`FOLLOWUP_REASONS` const array — the engine + Zod side), and (3) `db/migrations/029_create_followup_log.sql`'s `reason text CHECK` constraint. Adding a new reason without coordinating all three either silently fails at the DB (CHECK rejects the row at claim time) or trips the `FOLLOWUP_REASONS` round-trip test in `followup-rules.test.ts`. The round-trip test against the CHECK enumeration is the canary; a new reason ships with a migration that widens the CHECK and a test update that bumps the assertion.
- **TAC-123 `followup_log.dedup_key` construction is reason-appropriate and centralized in `dedupKeyForReason`.** `post_visit_day_N` and `cold_lapsed` key on `last_visit_at_iso` (so a fresh visit produces a new key → auto re-arm); `perk_unlock` keys on `mechanic_id` (so different perks dedup independently). The helper throws on argument-mismatch (e.g., `post_visit_*` without `lastVisitAt`) — engine wiring bug, fail fast. **Do not roll your own dedup_key construction at a new call site;** the engine, the claim helpers, and the `loadFollowupSnapshotsForVenue` perk-set parser all assume the same format, and a custom key risks duplicating sends or missing the re-arm semantic.
- **TAC-123 engine sends always persist as `messages.category='follow_up'` regardless of which reasons fire.** Plan-review call: the perk-weaving clause lives in the `follow_up` category instruction (`lib/ai/prompts/categories/follow-up.ts`); routing perk-bearing engine runs to `category='perk_unlock'` would mean that clause never applied. The existing `perk_unlock` MessageCategory remains for the inbound / standalone perk moment — different tuning. `triggerToCategory(perk_unlock) = 'follow_up'` in `lib/agent/handle-followup.ts`. The perk detail itself rides as `perkBeingUnlocked` + a `'perk_unlock'` entry in `FollowupContext.reasons[]`.
- **TAC-123 `messaging_cadence` vs `followup_rules` split is intentional — DON'T duplicate fields.** `venue_configs.messaging_cadence` carries ONLY the pure day-schedule (`{day_1, day_3, day_7, day_14}`); `venue_configs.followup_rules` carries everything else (gates, cap, dedup windows, quiet hours, cron hour, per-reason kill switches). Both jsonb, both read by the engine. The original messaging_cadence column has existed since migration 001 but was UNREAD at runtime until TAC-123; the engine is the first reader. If you reach for "let's just stuff cron_hour_local into messaging_cadence" the split breaks — messaging_cadence is referenced in the operator-facing seed flow and in narrow ways elsewhere; keep the slimmer schema.
- **TAC-123 claim-before-side-effect is load-bearing for double-send safety.** GH Actions scheduled runs can lag the target minute, and overlapping runs would otherwise both pass the dedup read and both dispatch. The flow MUST be: `claimFollowupLogRows` (atomic multi-row INSERT — any UNIQUE violation rolls the whole statement back, surfaces as `error.code='23505'`, helper returns `{conflict: true}`) BEFORE `handleFollowup`, then either finalize, release, or keep-claim per the failure-stage policy below. A future refactor that "simplifies" by moving the INSERT to post-dispatch reopens the double-send race the operator's plan-review specifically called out. **Do NOT add `.onConflict('do_nothing')`** to the helper either — see the inline assertion comment in `claimFollowupLogRows`; the atomic-rollback semantic is what makes the engine's "skip whole guest on conflict" correct. If you ever need partial-claim semantics, add the explicit count comparison + release-of-inserted-rows branch AND update the migration 029 header to match.
- **TAC-123 post-persist failure keeps the claim row (audit, not retry).** When `handleFollowup` returns `{ status: 'failed', stage }` with `stage IN ('persist', 'send')` — Sendblue threw mid-dispatch, or the `scheduleAndSend` row insert succeeded but a follow-on UPDATE failed — the engine LEAVES the followup_log claim row in place with `message_id=NULL`. Releasing would let the next morning tick re-claim and re-dispatch, producing a duplicate guest text. Pre-persist stage failures (`context_build` / `classification` / `corpus` / `generation`) DO release (no side-effect occurred, safe retry). An unexpected throw from `handleFollowup` (the orchestrator is supposed to be fail-closed; this shouldn't happen) is treated as post-persist (keep claim) because we don't know what state the row is in. Operator-side audit query: `SELECT * FROM followup_log WHERE message_id IS NULL AND created_at < now() - interval '1 day'` — those are orphan claims that may need manual reconciliation. This is documented in `db/migrations/029_create_followup_log.sql` header step 7 and asserted in `engine.test.ts`.
- **`transactions.amount_cents` is nullable (TAC-323), and null must never be silently coerced to zero somewhere it changes meaning.** Reserved for one case: a guest-reported order where a resolved item has no price in `venue_info` — the WHOLE order's amount is null, never a partial sum (a partial sum looks complete and isn't). Two DIFFERENT rules apply depending on whether you're summing or displaying, and conflating them is the mistake to avoid: **aggregation contexts** (`lib/recognition/load-signals.ts`'s `totalSpentCents`, the admin conversations page's `spendCents90d`) treat null as a 0-contribution to the sum — this is SQL's `SUM()`-ignores-NULL semantics, not a claim the order cost $0, and it's fine. **Per-row display contexts** (`transaction-row.tsx`'s row-level amount, `parse-ticket.ts`'s per-line-item price) must NEVER do this — `null / 100` coerces to `0` in JS, which would render a fabricated "$0.00" for an order whose true amount is simply unknown. Both files render a muted "—" instead. **Averaging is the sharp edge**: the admin conversations page tracks a SEPARATE `pricedVisitCount90d` denominator (visit-days with at least one known-amount transaction) distinct from the headline `visitCount90d` (every visit-day, known or not) specifically so one unpriced guest-reported visit can't silently drag `avgPerVisitCents` down — a visit with an unknown-but-nonzero true spend averaged in as $0 deflates the figure. If you add a new `amount_cents` consumer, ask which of these two buckets it falls into before writing `?? 0`.
- **`parseTicket` keeps a line item with a valid name + quantity but no `unit_price_cents` (TAC-323) — this is a deliberate reversal of its prior "drop anything malformed" posture, for this one field only.** Pre-TAC-323, any line item missing `unit_price_cents` was dropped entirely (silently vanishing from the ticket). Now it's kept with `unitPriceCents: null`, and `transaction-row.tsx` renders a blank/"—" price cell for it. Reason: a guest-reported order can resolve to a real menu item that simply has no price in `venue_info` (a "market price" / `priceNote`-only item), and dropping that line would hide from the operator that the guest reported it at all — worse than showing an incomplete price. `name`/`quantity` are still required for the item to appear at all; only the price field got this relaxation.
- **`venue_info.qrEnrollmentMessage` (TAC-323) is deliberately NOT a new `venue_configs` column.** The QR-enrollment ticket's original plan proposed a bare new top-level text column; that was pushed back on during plan review as an unscoped schema change outside the repo's established per-venue-config-with-a-Zod-schema convention (`approval_policy`, `followup_rules`, `brand_persona`, `venue_info` are all one jsonb column each, with a dedicated parser). The enrollment string instead lives as one more optional field on the EXISTING `VenueInfoSchema` (`lib/schemas/venue-info.ts`) — no migration needed at all for it, since `venue_configs.venue_info` has existed since migration 001. The Sendblue webhook (`app/api/webhooks/sendblue/route.ts`) is the only reader: on a phone number with no existing guest at the venue, an exact match (both sides trimmed) between the inbound body and this field resolves `guests.created_via` to `'qr_scan'`; anything else resolves to `'inbound_message'` (replacing the pre-TAC-323 hardcoded `'manual'` placeholder). Fails safe to `'inbound_message'` on any lookup or parse error — losing the provenance label costs nothing functionally, so there's no reason to fail louder here.
- **TAC-323's order extractor is fire-and-forget by design, and the extracted order is NOT available to generation.** `lib/agent/extract-reported-order.ts` is dispatched via `waitUntil` from `handle-inbound.ts` right after classification succeeds, specifically so a slow or failed Haiku call can never delay or block the guest's reply. The accepted consequence: `generateStage` runs with no knowledge of what the extractor found (or whether it ran at all) on this same turn, so the reply body can never reference a just-recorded self-reported order. This is not a bug — don't "fix" it by threading the extraction result into the runtime context without re-opening the plan-review discussion; doing so would reintroduce the exact latency coupling the fire-and-forget design exists to avoid.
- **`resolveReportedItems` prices a duplicated menu-item name at the HIGHEST matching row, never drops it (TAC-323).** `MenuItemSchema` and the CSV parser both permit multiple rows sharing one `name` distinguished only by `size` — confirmed live on the Mock Sextant menu (five duplicated names; three disagree on price: `olipop`, `wild wonder peach ginger`, `vive immunity boost`, `topochico`, `hario v60 dripper`). Size-aware pricing is explicitly out of scope for this ticket, so there's no way to know which variant the guest meant — but the guest DID name a real menu item, and an earlier version of this code dropped the item entirely on price disagreement (treating it as unresolvable); that was overturned deliberately: losing an item the guest actually reported is worse than pricing it imprecisely, since `amount_cents` was already an estimate by design (`raw_data.amount_source: 'menu_estimate'`) and the item still counts toward `percentMenuExplored` either way. Rows with no price at all (`priceNote`-only) are excluded from the max; if every matching row is unpriced, the item resolves with a null `unitPriceCents`, same as a single unpriced item.
- **`bodyMentionsMenuItem` matches on individual significant WORDS, not the whole menu name — the original whole-name version shipped broken and passed every unit test (TAC-323).** The prefilter's job is "is it worth calling the model at all," and the first version tested whether the guest's message contained a menu item's FULL name as one normalized substring. That's correct for a single-word menu ("Cortado") — every one of the test suite's fixtures happened to be single-word — and silently dead for a real one: Mock Sextant's actual menu is 47 of 69 items multi-word or slash-separated ("Gibraltar / Cortado", "Wild Wonder Peach Ginger"), and no guest types the full canonical name verbatim. First live UAT reply ("i got an oat cortado and a croissant") wrote zero transactions — the gate returned `no_menu_item_mentioned` before the Haiku call ever fired, and every unit test still passed because none of them modeled a real multi-word name. **The generalization: a prefilter/gate test suite built entirely from short, single-token fixtures proves nothing about a matcher's behavior on real multi-word data — write at least one fixture shaped like production before trusting the green suite,** the same lesson as this file's other "the test passed and the behavior was still wrong" entries (`comp_regex_backstop`, the push `future-add safety` test). Fix: `extractSignificantWords` splits each menu name on whitespace/`/`/punctuation (alphanumeric runs stay whole — `v60` isn't split into `v` + `60`), drops a short stopword list and anything under 3 characters, and the prefilter hits if ANY of a menu item's significant words is a **boundary-checked** match (`bodyContainsWord`, TAC-326 — see below; was raw substring containment through TAC-324) against the normalized body. Deliberately biased toward over-triggering — a false positive here costs one cheap Haiku call, a false negative silently kills the feature for that message, and the accepted-tradeoff framing ("a cheapness gate, not a correctness gate") is unchanged; only the unit of comparison moved from whole-name to word.
  **A second code-review pass on this exact fix — requested specifically because the first version had shipped broken and green — found two more members of the same failure family before either reached production, both now fixed in the same function:** (1) a menu item stored in PLURAL form ("Croissants") was silently unmatchable against the guest's natural singular phrasing ("i got a croissant") — the reverse direction already worked via plain substring containment, only plural-menu/singular-guest was broken. Fixed with a naive (non-lemmatizing) de-pluralization step: for a word ending "s", also try it minus one trailing char; for a word ending "es", also try it minus two — deliberately generating BOTH candidates when applicable rather than picking one rule, since a wrong extra candidate only costs one more cheap Haiku call while under-covering leaves a real plural silently unmatchable again. (2) The split regex treats any non-ASCII character as a separator, so an accented name ("Piña", "Crème Brûlée") fragments into pieces that can ALL die at the 3-char floor, leaving the item permanently unmatchable by any phrasing — real risk for this product's target market (independent cafes/bakeries routinely carry accented names). Fixed with `stripDiacritics` (NFD normalize + strip the U+0300-U+036F combining-marks block) applied to both the menu name and the guest's message before either is split/compared. `MENU_WORD_STOPWORDS` was also trimmed from nine entries to four (`the`, `and`, `with`, `for`) — `of`/`in`/`a`/`an`/`to` were dead code, since the 3-char length floor already excluded them before the stopword check could ever run; a stopword-list entry under the length floor is a signal the list wasn't checked against what it actually does, not a hedge.
  **A third round (TAC-326) found the same failure family again, this time via live UAT rather than code review, and this time with a second consumer exposed to it.** `bodyMentionsMenuItem`'s per-word check was plain, unanchored substring containment — a menu-derived word matched the moment it appeared anywhere inside a longer, unrelated word, with no concept of a boundary. Mock Sextant's menu includes "San Pellegrino" (significant word `san`), and a guest's literal first message, `"Hi Sana!"` — greeting the venue's own persona by name — silently matched, because `"hi sana!".includes("san")` is true. Not a one-off: the same menu's `"Hibiscus Ice Tea"` (word `ice`) is a substring of the ordinary word `"nice"`, arguably worse because "nice" is common guest chat rather than a venue-specific name. **This is exactly why `bodyMentionsMenuItem` now has TWO consumers with opposite risk profiles, and tuning it requires knowing which one you're protecting:** `extractReportedOrder` (TAC-323) tolerates a false positive (one wasted Haiku call, invisible) and is hurt by a false negative (permanently kills extraction for that message); `applyCurrentTurnSuppression` (`lib/agent/intentions/derive.ts`, TAC-324) is the reverse — a false positive there silently drops a rendered prompt line with nothing to signal it happened, a false negative just means the line renders once when it strictly didn't need to. The concatenation bug was invisible on the first consumer for two full ship cycles (TAC-323's own gate never surfaced a symptom) and only became visible once the second consumer gave a false positive a cost worth noticing. Fixed with `bodyContainsWord`: the word must be preceded by a true boundary (`[^a-z0-9]` or string-start) and followed by either a true boundary or exactly one recognized plural suffix (`s`/`es` — the same vocabulary `extractSignificantWords` already generates variants for, not a new concept) then a boundary. Verified against all pre-existing test cases (none regress) plus both live collisions (both now correctly non-matching) plus explicit sanity checks in `lib/agent/extract-reported-order.test.ts` that genuine mentions still match, including genuine plurals, a real hyphenated menu item ("Carrot-Orange Juice"), and a possessive-suffixed mention ("the latte's great today") — hyphen and apostrophe were already outside `[a-z0-9]`, so they were always boundaries, never at risk of being glued into a word. The asymmetry above is now written directly into the function's own doc comment, not just here, specifically so a future person tuning it doesn't have to re-derive which direction is safe.
  **A structurally different, larger problem was found in the same pass and deliberately NOT fixed here: single-word matching on a multi-word proper noun.** Boundary-checking does not and cannot stop `"san"` matching a genuine, correctly-boundaried mention of `"San Francisco"` — there's no boundary violation there, just semantic ambiguity about which "san"-prefixed thing is meant. Every cheap fix considered (require the full phrase; require ≥2 significant words for multi-word names; raise the standalone-match length floor) breaks an existing, deliberately-shipped single-word match from the rounds above — `"grabbed a ginger drink"` matching `"Wild Wonder Peach Ginger"` on `ginger` alone, `"cortado"` alone matching the slash-alternate-names `"Gibraltar / Cortado"`, or the `v60` case (also exactly 3 characters, would be caught by any length-floor raise that also excludes `san`/`ice`). Deferred deliberately, not silently: `lib/agent/intentions/derive.test.ts`'s regression corpus asserts CURRENT (still-suppressing) behavior for a San Francisco-style mention, with a comment naming the deferral so a future "fix" doesn't reopen the Sana/nice bugs by loosening the boundary check instead of building the distinctiveness model this actually needs. **The reasoning for why it's safe to defer, and why the SAME reasoning does NOT extend to the case below, is turn-scoping:** `applyCurrentTurnSuppression` writes no prompt row and doesn't close the intention, so an incidental false positive on an ordinary message costs exactly one turn's rendering — `learn_first_order` derives open again on the next turn that doesn't also collide.
  **Turn-scoping is exactly what breaks for the QR prefilled body, and that's why TAC-326 treats it as a separate, non-deferrable requirement.** `venue_info.qrEnrollmentMessage` (TAC-323) is not an ordinary organic message among many — it's a static, per-venue-configured string guaranteed to be the guest's literal first-ever inbound, every time, for every guest at that venue. If it collides with that venue's own menu, there is no later, non-colliding turn to recover on, because the first-touch-intentions mechanism (TAC-324) is specifically about that one turn. Mock Sextant's actual live config IS this case: `qrEnrollmentMessage = "Hi Sana!"`, colliding with its own `"San Pellegrino"` — confirmed directly against the live DB, not assumed; it's the reason the bug was caught at all. Guarded by a test in `lib/agent/extract-reported-order.test.ts` (`QR prefilled-body collision guard`): a hardcoded snapshot of each known venue's `qrEnrollmentMessage` + full menu, asserting `bodyMentionsMenuItem` returns false, plus a deliberately-colliding fixture proving the guard actually fires rather than being green only because nothing collides today. Deliberately the cheap version, not the elegant one — a pure, CI-safe unit test over a hand-maintained table, not a live query (this repo's tests don't hit external services) and not a seed-time validation. **Staleness risk, accepted deliberately:** if a venue's `qrEnrollmentMessage` or menu changes in Studio without this table being updated to match, a new collision could go uncaught. The elegant version — a check inside `scripts/onboarding/seed-supabase.ts` or `scripts/seed-venue.ts` that runs automatically whenever a venue's config is (re-)seeded, catching this for every future venue without anyone remembering to update a fixture by hand — is a known, named follow-up, not built here.
  **The resolver (`resolveReportedItems`) is unaffected by any of the above** — it uses a DIFFERENT, whole-name normalization (`normalizeMenuItemName`) and isn't doing the prefilter's job; it's checking whether the model's *returned* name is a real menu item. That check is now STRUCTURAL, not prompt-based: `lib/ai/extract-reported-order.ts` (v1.2.0) builds the output schema's `name` field as `z.enum(menuItemNames)` PER CALL (deduped, keyed on the venue's actual menu at request time) instead of a bare `z.string()` + a prompt instruction to "return it exactly" — the same code review that found (1) and (2) above called out, correctly, that a prompt instruction is not a substitute for a structural constraint when the valid-values list is already known at request time, and that a `z.string()` version would silently accept a hallucinated or subtly-reformatted (extra whitespace, different slash spacing, curly-vs-straight apostrophe) name that `normalizeMenuItemName`'s exact-match would then drop one layer down with zero visibility into why. `extractReportedOrder` (the Haiku call) also gained its own empty-menu short-circuit (`z.enum` requires a non-empty tuple in its TS type, though this repo's Zod version doesn't actually throw on `z.enum([])` at construction — the guard exists anyway, unconditionally BEFORE the schema is built, so the question is moot in practice) independent of `bodyMentionsMenuItem`'s existing empty-menu guard, so the AI module defends its own contract regardless of caller discipline. **Verified, not assumed, that the enum's dedup doesn't leak into the max-price rule:** the dedup in `lib/ai/extract-reported-order.ts` operates on a local `string[]` built for enum construction only (`[...new Set(input.menuItemNames)]`) — it never touches the `MenuItem[]` array the orchestrator holds. Twelve "Olipop" rows at different prices collapse to ONE enum entry (the model can only ever return "Olipop" once per item, correctly), but `resolveReportedItems` still receives and groups all twelve original rows, so the max-price rule still sees every price. A cross-module composition test in `lib/ai/extract-reported-order.test.ts` (importing the REAL `resolveReportedItems`, not a mock) exercises exactly this.
